                                                                                         Synthesis
The invariant language has three algebraic axes. Transverse order is an E1 structure for ⊗! . Associative
chirality is an E1 structure for ★ch . Chiral Lie theory is a Lie structure for ★ch . Their bars are Bar⊥ , Barch
                                                                                                               Ass ,
         ch
and BarLie . The aligned projector supplies the exact sector on which a mixed distributive law is defined.
Every comparison in the theorem system factors through one of these typed maps.
   The constructed operations form the two functorial rows

                                     Obs𝜕                          Barord
           HT boundary theory −−−−→ AlgE1 (Fact𝐷 ( 𝑋 )) −−−−−→ Coalgconil,cpl (Fact𝐷 ( 𝑋 )) ,

                                                       𝑍 ord
                                AlgE1 (Fact𝐷 ( 𝑋 )) −−−−→ AlgE2 (Fact𝐷 ( 𝑋 )) .
A primitive Lie algebra enters when a separate Chevalley or Hall comparison identifies the bar coalgebra
with the Chevalley chains of that Lie algebra. Hall geometry supplies this comparison through extension
correspondences. Quantum groups require an additional coproduct and exchange structure. Automor-
phic forms arise from graded Euler characters after a grading and completion have been fixed. Each arrow
therefore preserves the geometric operation named at its source.

                                                                                    CHAPTER
                                                                                                            42
Concordance with the July 2026 Volume I
                                   draft
The present volume preserves the draft’s five-carrier geometry, transverse bar construction, mixed-forest
signs, and noncommutative example ladder. It sharpens four interfaces:
   (i) a factorization carrier is paired explicitly with its operation;
  (ii) the Ran category is fixed before its tensor products are used;
 (iii) the block-ordered operad is introduced after a representing chiral operad is chosen;
 (iv) ribbon and stable-curve sewing are derived from nondegenerate Calabi–Yau and modular data.
These refinements make every theorem a statement in a fixed category and every physical interpretation
the image of a displayed comparison map.

       The enveloping bar and the audit of the
                                 planar tables
The three bars of the typed bar theorem are computed here on enveloping algebras, and the computation
settles two dimension formulas that circulated as bar cohomology.

43.1    Chevalley comparison
Theorem 43.1 (Bar–Chevalley comparison). Let 𝔤 be a Lie algebra object in FactD( 𝑋 ) for the pointwise
product, 𝐾 -ﬂat, positively graded and complete, with every total weight admitting ﬁnitely many decomposi-
tions into positive weights, and suppose internal PBW holds for 𝑈 (𝔤). There is a natural quasi-isomorphism
of conilpotent coalgebras in FactD( 𝑋 )
                                                      ∼
                                           Bar⊥ 𝑈 (𝔤) −−→ 𝐶∗ (𝔤) ,

compatible with the factorization coeﬃcient and the PBW ﬁltration.
Proof. Filter 𝑈 (𝔤) by PBW degree and both complexes by total word length. The associated graded enveloping
algebra is Sym(𝔤), and the bar of a symmetric algebra is the suspended symmetric coalgebra on 𝔤, which is the
associated graded of Chevalley chains. The comparison is an isomorphism on the first page; finite decomposition
gives a bounded exhaustive spectral sequence in each weight, and completeness promotes it to a quasi-isomorphism.
Every step uses only internal tensor products and the bracket, so the map stays in FactD( 𝑋 ).

Calculation 43.2 (Finite-dimensional simple case). For a simple Lie algebra of rank 𝑟 with exponents
𝑚1 , . . . , 𝑚𝑟 , the cohomology is the exterior algebra on primitive classes of degrees 2𝑚𝑖 + 1:
   Bar⊥ 𝑈 (𝔰𝔩 2 ) : 1 + 𝑡 3 ,     Bar⊥ 𝑈 (𝔰𝔩 3 ) : 1 + 𝑡 3 + 𝑡 5 + 𝑡 8 ,   Bar⊥ 𝑈 (𝔤2 ) : 1 + 𝑡 3 + 𝑡 11 + 𝑡 14 .

The first two were recomputed by exact rational elimination on Λ•𝔤∗ built from matrix units, with 𝑑 2 = 0
asserted on every cochain space rather than assumed, giving Betti numbers 1, 0, 0, 1 and 1, 0, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0, 0, 1.
The Chevalley chain dimensions are dim   𝑛 , so for 𝔰𝔩 2 they are 1, 3, 3, 1 while the bar chain dimensions of
                                          𝔤

an augmented algebra with dim 𝐴¯ = 3 are 3𝑟 .

43.3      The planar tables
Two formulas circulated as bar cohomology dimensions: dim 𝐻 𝑛 = 𝑀 ( 𝑛 + 1) − 𝑀 ( 𝑛) =
1, 2, 5, 12, 30, 76, . . . for the Virasoro family, with 𝑀 the Motzkin numbers, and dim 𝐻 𝑛 = 𝑅 ( 𝑛 + 3) =
3, 6, 15, 36, 91, . . . for the 𝔰𝔩 2 family, with 𝑅 the Riordan numbers. The coincidence of the discriminant
√
   1 − 2 𝑧 − 3 𝑧 2 in the two generating functions was attributed to Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction.

Proposition 43.4 (The two sequences are one sequence). For all 𝑛 ≥ 0,

                𝑀 ( 𝑛) = 𝑅 ( 𝑛) + 𝑅 ( 𝑛 + 1) ,       𝑀 ( 𝑛 + 1) − 𝑀 ( 𝑛) = 𝑅 ( 𝑛 + 2) − 𝑅 ( 𝑛) .

The Virasoro sequence is therefore 𝑅 ( 𝑛 + 2) − 𝑅 ( 𝑛) and the 𝔰𝔩 2 sequence is 𝑅 ( 𝑛 + 3): both are elementary
transforms of one sequence, and their generating functions are rational in the single algebraic function
√
  1 − 2 𝑧 − 3 𝑧 2 for that reason alone. No Drinfeld–Sokolov input is used or needed.
Proof. Riordan numbers count Motzkin paths with no level step at height zero. Splitting a Motzkin path of length
𝑛 according to whether its first step is level gives 𝑀 ( 𝑛) = 𝑅 ( 𝑛) + 𝑅 ( 𝑛 + 1); subtracting consecutive instances
gives the second identity. Both were checked termwise for 𝑛 < 24.

Theorem 43.5 (Carrier theorem for the planar series). Neither sequence is the ordered bar homology of
the algebra it names, and neither is a bar chain count.
      (i) For 𝔰𝔩 2 the bar homology is 1, 0, 0, 1 by Calculation 43.2, and the bar chain dimensions are
          3, 9, 27, 81, 243. The sequence 3, 6, 15, 36, 91 is neither.
  (ii) For the Virasoro family the bar homology is the constant 2 by Theorem 43.3. On degrees 𝑛 = 1, 2, 3
       the asserted values are 1, 2, 5; they agree with the truth at 𝑛 = 2 and nowhere else.


114
115                                                                             43.3. The planar tables


  (iii) Independently of any presentation, a Virasoro chiral coeﬃcient does not determine the transverse bar:
        a ﬁxed coeﬃcient may be combined with free, square-zero, enveloping, or quantum-plane transverse
        algebras whose bars differ, while the coeﬃcient and the central charge are unchanged.
Consequently a chain-level identiﬁcation of either planar series with an ordered bar requires a speciﬁed
transverse multiplication together with a quasi-isomorphism from its planar complex to that bar, and by
(i) and (ii) no such quasi-isomorphism exists for the algebras named.

Remark 43.6 (The type error beneath the numerical one). The bar homology of 𝑈 ( 𝐿1 ) is bigraded by
cohomological degree and weight, and is finite in each degree only because Theorem 43.3 concentrates it
in two weights. A formula assigning one integer to each degree, with no weight truncation named, does
not specify an invariant of a graded object with infinite-dimensional graded pieces.




                                                                                                          115
                                                                                              CHAPTER
                                                                                                                        44
                                   The obstruction constitution
Each statement below names what is not determined, and therefore what must be supplied. Without
them the preceding theorems admit false corollaries.

Proposition 44.1 (Where a mixed distributive law can live). The sector on which a mixed distributive law
between ⊗! and ★ch is geometrically deﬁned is the common-decomposition sector, the image of the aligned
idempotent 𝑒al . Off that sector the two products admit no interchange, only the aligned retraction, so no
distributive law can be written.
Proof. A distributive law requires a natural transformation interchanging the two products in a fixed direction,
subject to the coherence conditions of each. The canonical comparison in the additive model is the aligned inclusion
𝜄 with retraction 𝜋 , and 𝜋 𝜄 = id while 𝜄𝜋 = 𝑒al ≠ id in general. A natural transformation subject to both coherence
conditions therefore exists only where 𝑒al acts as the identity.

   This locates a mixed law without constructing one. No example in the present corpus exhibits one;
Zamolodchikov–Faddeev algebras are the natural candidate, since their spectral variable belongs to the
chiral direction while their word belongs to the transverse direction.

Theorem 44.2 (No quantum group from dimension loss). The dimension of the kernel of 𝑉 ⊗ 𝑛 →
Sym𝑛 𝑉 determines no 𝑅-matrix, associator, or quantum group. That kernel carries no multiplication,
coproduct, monodromy, or coherence equation.
Proof. The kernel is a representation of Σ𝑛 and nothing more. Two algebras with the same underlying graded
vector space, hence the same kernel dimensions in every arity, may have different multiplications and inequivalent
representation categories; a free algebra and a quadratic quotient with the same Hilbert series is an example. A
quantum-group structure is a coproduct together with coherence data, none of which is a function of a dimension.


Theorem 44.3 (No modular object from topology alone). The identity 𝜕 2 = 0 on M 𝑔,𝑛 assigns no
coeﬃcient operations to boundary strata. A modular master element exists only after cyclic contractions and
clutching-compatible coeﬃcient maps have been supplied.

Theorem 44.4 (No bulk from a boundary centre). The derived centre is terminal among algebraic
central actions on the boundary algebra. It does not detect a decoupled bulk sector, so a physical bulk is not
determined by the boundary chiral algebra unless a bulk-to-centre comparison is supplied and proved to be
an equivalence.



                                                                                                                 117
44. The obstruction constitution                                                                                  118


Proof. Terminality is the universal property of the centre. It is a statement about the boundary category and is
insensitive to any sector acting trivially on it, so a bulk with trivial boundary action is invisible.

Theorem 44.5 (No classification by central charge). Central charge determines the Virasoro anomaly
coeﬃcient. It does not determine the chiral operation, the representation category, the braid monodromy, or
the higher-genus conformal blocks. Distinct holomorphic theories at equal central charge exist.

Theorem 44.6 (No identification of anomalies of different types). A current-algebra level, a Virasoro
central charge, a determinant-line curvature, a BRST ghost-number anomaly, and a Borcherds-product
weight are invariants in different theories, living in different deformation complexes. They may be related
by a theorem in a particular family. They are not one universal scalar, and no identity among them follows
from numerical agreement.
Proof. Each is a class in a distinct complex: an Atiyah-algebra extension class, a Virasoro cocycle, the curvature of
a determinant line, a class in 𝐻 1 or 𝐻 2 of a BRST convolution complex, and a weight in the character lattice of an
automorphic form. A map between two of these complexes is additional data; in its absence, equality of numbers
is a coincidence of values, not an identification of classes.

Theorem 44.7 (No unnamed comparison of bars). An asserted equality Bar⊥ ( 𝐴) = Barch       Ass ( 𝐴) has
no meaning without a functor carrying the pointwise multiplication problem to the chiral-convolution
multiplication problem. The two have different underlying gradings, counting interval subdivisions and
collision trees respectively. Agreement of Euler characteristics is not a comparison map.

Theorem 44.8 (A scalar sequence is not homology data). A sequence of integers becomes bar homology only
after a chain complex, a differential, a grading convention, and a rank computation or quasi-isomorphism
are speciﬁed. Matching initial values, discriminants, or growth rates constructs no such map.
Proof. Chain-space dimensions and homology dimensions differ by the ranks of the differentials, which are
not functions of the chain dimensions. Calculation 43.2 exhibits an algebra whose bar chain dimensions are
3, 9, 27, 81, 243 and whose bar homology dimensions are 1, 0, 0, 1; Proposition 43.4 exhibits two sequences with
the same discriminant and growth rate that are transforms of one another and of neither homology.

    Theorem 44.6 together with the central-charge statement retracts any programme treating a tuple of
scalars drawn from these sources as a single classifying invariant, and any trace identity asserted among
them without a named comparison morphism.

45.1   Hamiltonians and the Poisson bracket
Let 𝑉 = ℂ2 with coordinates ( 𝑧1 , 𝑧2 ) and symplectic form

                                                𝜔 = d 𝑧1 ∧ d 𝑧2 .

Let 𝔪 = ( 𝑧1 , 𝑧2 ). The pointed formal Hamiltonian Lie algebra is

                       𝔥 = 𝔪2 ⊂ ℂ⟦ 𝑧1 , 𝑧2 ⟧,         { 𝑓 , 𝑔 } = 𝜕𝑧1 𝑓 𝜕𝑧2 𝑔 − 𝜕𝑧2 𝑓 𝜕𝑧1 𝑔.

It consists of Hamiltonians whose vector fields fix the chosen Darboux point. The shifted order filtration
𝐹 𝑝 𝔥 = 𝔪 𝑝+2 is complete and obeys

                                           { 𝐹 𝑝 𝔥, 𝐹 𝑞 𝔥} ⊂ 𝐹 𝑝+𝑞 𝔥 .

The affine Hamiltonian algebra 𝔥aff = ℂ⟦ 𝑧1 , 𝑧2 ⟧/ℂ also contains translations. Its linear sector is adjoined
separately when a moving brane or center-of-mass coordinate is required.
   Write 𝑅𝑟 = Sym𝑟 (𝑉 ∨ ) for the homogeneous polynomials of degree 𝑟 . The bracket obeys

                                            { 𝑅𝑟 , 𝑅 𝑠 } ⊂ 𝑅𝑟 + 𝑠 −2 .

Lie algebra. Its dimension is

45.2    Unimodularity
Theorem 45.2 (Unimodularity of every finite jet). For every 𝑑 ≥ 2 and every 𝑥 ∈ 𝔥≤ 𝑑 ,

                                                  Tr𝔥≤ 𝑑 (ad 𝑥 ) = 0.

Proof. A homogeneous Hamiltonian of degree greater than two raises polynomial degree. Its adjoint action is
strictly upper triangular in the decomposition 𝑟𝑑=2 𝑅𝑟 , hence has trace zero. A quadratic Hamiltonian belongs
                                                    L
to 𝔰𝔩 2 . Its action on each finite-dimensional 𝔰𝔩 2 -module 𝑅𝑟 has trace zero. Linearity gives the result.

Corollary 45.3 (Canonical finite BV measure). The linear odd symplectic space 𝑇 ∗ [−1]𝔥≤ 𝑑 [1] has a
translation-invariant Berezinian for which the cotangent BF Hamiltonian satisﬁes the quantum master
equation.
Proof. Choose dual linear coordinates on the shifted cotangent space and use their constant Berezinian. The
classical term vanishes by Jacobi, while the BV divergence is the adjoint trace. Unimodularity makes that trace
zero.

                                The universal boundary brane
47.1    Polarization
Place the cotangent theory on a half-plane. The standard Lagrangian boundary condition fixes the
cotangent field and leaves the 𝔥≤ 𝑑 field free. Boundary observables form the Rees enveloping algebra

                              𝑈ℏ (𝔥≤ 𝑑 ) = 𝑇 (𝔥≤ 𝑑 )⟦ℏ⟧/⟨ 𝑥𝑦 − 𝑦𝑥 − ℏ[ 𝑥, 𝑦 ]⟩ .

The Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt symmetrization gives a filtered vector-space isomorphism
                                                               ∼
                                    symℏ : Sym(𝔥≤ 𝑑 )⟦ℏ⟧ −
                                                         → 𝑈ℏ (𝔥≤ 𝑑 ) .

Theorem 47.1 (Boundary quantization). The factorization algebra of boundary observables of the ﬁnite
cotangent theory is locally constant and its value on an interval is 𝑈ℏ (𝔥≤ 𝑑 ). Fusion of adjacent intervals is
multiplication in the enveloping algebra.
Proof. Canonical quantization turns the linear boundary phase space 𝔥∗≤ 𝑑 into the Gutt star algebra. For a linear
Poisson bracket the Gutt product is transported by PBW to 𝑈ℏ (𝔥≤ 𝑑 ). Excision identifies ordered interval fusion
with multiplication.

gives a filtered L∞ quasi-isomorphism from polyvectors with differential [ 𝜋𝑑 , −] to Hochschild cochains
of the star algebra. Tamarkin’s refinement transports the full homotopy Gerstenhaber structure.

a homotopy Swiss-cheese action associated with that reﬁnement. Its binary closed bracket is the Poisson bracket,

Theorem 50.1 (Continuous formal-Darboux equivalence). A stable formality morphism can be chosen
compatibly with the quotient maps 𝔥≤ 𝑑+1 → 𝔥≤ 𝑑 . It induces a continuous homotopy-E2 quasi-isomorphism
                                                       ∼
                                                                                   
                      Φ∞ : 𝐶CE∗         ∗                  •
                                                
                                ,cont 𝑇   [−1]𝔥   ⟦ℏ⟧ −
                                                      →  𝐶 HH,cont  𝑈bℏ (𝔥) , 𝑈

A compatible Swiss-cheese reﬁnement gives the full formal mixed holomorphic–topological open–closed
structure of the pointed Hamiltonian BF sector. Its cohomological Gerstenhaber structure is independent of
the chosen chain-level reﬁnement.
Proof. The Taylor coefficients of a stable formality morphism are universal finite sums of graphs. In any fixed
polynomial weight, finitely many jets and graphs contribute. The maps therefore commute with the jet projections.
The finite complexes form Mittag–Leffler towers in each weight, so inverse limit preserves the finite-level quasi-
isomorphisms. Brace operations are defined by finite insertion in every weight and pass to the same limit.

Principle 50.2 (Universal brane principle). The boundary algebra 𝑈bℏ (𝔥) is the universal formal Hamil-
tonian brane. Finite matrix branes are representation-theoretic images of this algebra. The bulk is its
complete derived centre.

                                             𝔥≤3  𝔰𝔩 2 ⋉ Sym3 (ℂ2 ) ,

where the second summand is abelian.
Proof. The bracket of two cubic polynomials has degree four and vanishes in the degree-three quotient. The
bracket with a quadratic polynomial is the displayed 𝔰𝔩 2 action.

Write 𝑤 𝑘 = 𝑧14− 𝑘 𝑧2𝑘 , 0 ≤ 𝑘 ≤ 4. In 𝔥≤4 the cubic modes satisfy

Calculation 51.2 (Two brackets).

                        { 𝑧13 , 𝑧23 } = 9 𝑧12 𝑧22 = 9𝑤2 ,   { 𝑧12 𝑧2 , 𝑧1 𝑧22 } = 3 𝑧12 𝑧22 = 3𝑤2 .

The two independent cubic pairs reach the same quartic channel with amplitudes in the ratio 3 : 1.
Proof. Differentiate each monomial in the two symplectic coordinates and substitute in the Poisson formula. The
first pair gives 3 𝑧12 · 3 𝑧22 . The second gives 2 𝑧1 𝑧2 · 2 𝑧1 𝑧2 − 𝑧12 · 𝑧22 = 3 𝑧12 𝑧22 .

Theorem 51.3 (The quartic jet presentation). The Lie algebra 𝔥≤4 is generated by 𝑒, 𝑓 , ℎ, 𝑣0 , 𝑣1 , 𝑣2 , 𝑣3 .
Its relations are the 𝔰𝔩 2 relations, the irreducible action on the 𝑣𝑗 , formula (51.3), and the induced 𝔰𝔩 2 action
on 𝑅4 . The quartic subspace is abelian in the quotient.
Proof. Quadratic and cubic generators produce every quartic monomial through (51.3). Brackets involving a
quartic and a cubic have degree five, and brackets of two quartics have degree six, so both vanish after projection.
Jacobi follows from the polynomial Poisson bracket before projection.

Theorem 52.1 (Quadratic exactness). For polynomials 𝑓 , 𝑔 of degree at most two,

                                                [ 𝑓 , 𝑔 ] ★ = ℏ{ 𝑓 , 𝑔 } .

Proof. The commutator retains the odd powers of the Poisson bidifferential. Derivatives of order three vanish on
quadratics, so the first-order term is the entire commutator.

The continuous dual of ℂ⟦ 𝑧1 , 𝑧2 ⟧ is the direct sum of the 𝜌 𝑎𝑏 . The continuous dual of the pointed
algebra 𝔥 = 𝔪2 is the span of the modes with 𝑎 + 𝑏 ≥ 2. The two modes of total degree one pair with
the translation sector of 𝔥aff .

Proposition 54.1 (Coadjoint current formula). For 𝑓 = 𝑧1𝑖 𝑧2 and 𝜌 𝑎𝑏 ,
                                                                         𝑗


                                        ad∗𝑓 ( 𝜌 𝑎𝑏 ) ( 𝑔 ) = − 𝜌 𝑎𝑏 ({ 𝑓 , 𝑔 }) .

In the residue basis this is a ﬁnite linear combination of modes of bidegree

                                              ( 𝑎 − 𝑖 + 1, 𝑏 − 𝑗 + 1) .

Proof. The definition is the coadjoint action. The Poisson bracket lowers the total degree of 𝑓 𝑔 by two, so residue
conservation fixes the displayed bidegree. The Poisson formula contains two derivative terms.

   The shifted cotangent current algebra

is therefore a complete topological Lie algebra with a continuous invariant pairing. Its finite jet quotients

                                                                                                                145
                                                                                              CHAPTER
                                                                                                                       55
                         All-arity open–closed operations
55.1   Hochschild braces
For Hochschild cochains 𝐹 ∈ 𝐶 𝑝 ( 𝐴, 𝐴) and 𝐺𝑖 ∈ 𝐶 𝑞𝑖 ( 𝐴, 𝐴), the brace
                                                  𝐹 {𝐺1 , . . . , 𝐺𝑟 }
is the signed sum over ordered insertions of the 𝐺𝑖 into the inputs of 𝐹 . The brace identities state that a
nested insertion is the sum over all ways of distributing the inner insertions among the outer slots.
Theorem 55.1 (Brace realization of the universal centre). For 𝐴 = 𝑈ℏ (𝔥≤ 𝑑 ), choose the Tamarkin–
Kontsevich homotopy-Gerstenhaber reﬁnement of Φ𝑑 . It transports the homotopy Schouten operations of
polyvector ﬁelds to the Hochschild brace algebra. The binary operation is the Gerstenhaber bracket, and the
mixed operation with one boundary word is the quantized Hamiltonian action.
Proof. Kontsevich formality is an algebra over a cofibrant resolution of the Gerstenhaber operad. Tamarkin’s
formality identifies that resolution with the brace operad. Twisting by the linear Poisson Maurer–Cartan element
produces the differential and preserves every brace operation.

55.2    The first mixed identities
Let 𝐹 be a closed cochain and 𝑎, 𝑏 boundary operators. The first Swiss-cheese identities are
                                        𝐹 · ( 𝑎𝑏 ) = ( 𝐹(1) · 𝑎) ( 𝐹(2) · 𝑏 ) ,
for the coproduct encoded by the brace diagonal, and
                             [ 𝐹 , 𝐺 ] · 𝑎 = 𝐹 · ( 𝐺 · 𝑎) − (−1) | 𝐹 | | 𝐺 | 𝐺 · ( 𝐹 · 𝑎) .
For linear Hamiltonians these are the Leibniz rule and the commutator identity of derivations. Higher
braces provide their coherent homotopies.
Corollary 55.2 (All-arity formal-Darboux theorem). The inverse limit Φ∞ equips the pair
                                                                
                                         ∗
                                        𝐶CE (𝑇 ∗ [−1]𝔥) , 𝑈
                                                          bℏ (𝔥)

with a complete homotopy Swiss-cheese algebra. Its closed color is the full derived centre of the universal
boundary algebra.
Proof. Every fixed polynomial weight receives finitely many brace insertions, so the finite-level Swiss-cheese oper-
ations commute with the jet projections. Their separated inverse limit gives the complete action. Morita theory
identifies Hochschild cochains of the boundary algebra with its derived centre.



                                                                                                               147
                                                                                           CHAPTER
                                                                                                         56

                                                Finite Chan–Paton ranks
56.1    Rank one
For 𝑁 = 1, the commuting equation is automatic. The boundary coordinate algebra is ℂ[ 𝑥, 𝑦 ], and

                                                   𝐽1 ( 𝑓 ) = 𝑓 ( 𝑥, 𝑦 ) .

The necklace bracket reduces to the ordinary Poisson bracket. Restriction to 𝑓 ∈ 𝔪2 gives the defining
pointed-Hamiltonian representation. The two linear Hamiltonians act by translations and recover the
affine center-of-mass extension.

56.2     Rank two and Cayley–Hamilton reduction
For a 2 × 2 matrix 𝑋 ,
                                         𝑋 2 − (Tr 𝑋 ) 𝑋 + (det 𝑋 ) 𝐼 = 0.
Hence every trace containing two consecutive powers of 𝑋 reduces to traces with 𝑋 -degree at most one
after adjoining Tr 𝑋 and det 𝑋 . The same statement holds for 𝑌 . On the commuting locus, simultaneous
diagonalization identifies the invariant algebra with the symmetric polynomials in two ordered pairs.

Proposition 56.1 (First finite-rank trace relation). For commuting 2 × 2 matrices,
                                        2                                2
                                 𝑝20 = 𝑝10 − 2 det 𝑋 ,            𝑝02 = 𝑝01 − 2 det 𝑌 ,

and
                                                                         2
                                         𝑝112 − 𝑝20 𝑝02 = − 𝑥1 𝑦2 − 𝑥2 𝑦1 .
The stable generators remain independent until a relation forced by the rank is reached.
Proof. Write the joint eigenvalues as ( 𝑥1 , 𝑦1 ) and ( 𝑥2 , 𝑦2 ) and expand both sides.

56.3    Stable passage
The transition from rank 𝑁 to rank 𝑁 + 1 adds a zero eigenvalue. Each fixed weighted degree stabilizes
once 𝑁 exceeds that degree. The inverse limit is therefore the completed multisymmetric algebra. The
stable trace is an exact quotient of the universal centre on the polynomial trace sector.

                                                                       Fedosov descent
57.1    The Weyl algebroid
Let ( 𝑆, 𝜔) be a holomorphic symplectic surface. The bundle of formal Weyl algebras has fibre the
completed Weyl algebra of a formal symplectic plane. A holomorphic symplectic connection and a
Fedosov one-form define a flat connection
                                                       1
                                              𝐷 𝐹 = ∇ + [ 𝛾, −]
                                                       ℏ
whose flat sections form a deformation-quantization algebroid W𝑆,ℏ .

Theorem 57.1 (Globalized formality on a holomorphic symplectic surface). Fix a stable formality
morphism and a compatible homotopy-E2 reﬁnement. There is a sheafwise homotopy-E2 quasi-isomorphism
                                                            ∼
                             Φ 𝑆 : (PV 𝑆 ⟦ℏ⟧, [ℏ 𝜋, −]) −  •
                                                        → 𝐶HH (W𝑆,ℏ , W𝑆,ℏ ) ,

where 𝜋 = 𝜔 −1 . A change of holomorphic symplectic connection or Fedosov one-form gives a gauge-equivalent
resolution and a homotopic quasi-isomorphism. Holomorphic symplectomorphisms act equivariantly on
the resulting homotopy class.
Proof. Fedosov resolutions replace both sheaves by acyclic sheaves of formal fibrewise polyvectors and polydifferen-
tial operators. The fixed covariant formality morphism acts fibrewise and intertwines the Fedosov differentials. A
change of connection or Fedosov one-form is a gauge transformation in the pronilpotent resolution and therefore
gives a homotopy.

Corollary 57.2 (Global mixed-HT centre). The deformation-quantization category Perf (W𝑆,ℏ ) has
Morita centre
                        𝑍 Mor Perf (W𝑆,ℏ ) ≃ ℝΓ( 𝑆, PV 𝑆 ⟦ℏ⟧, [ℏ 𝜋, −]) .
                                          

For a K3 surface this gives a global open–closed equivalence for the Hamiltonian BF sector.
Proof. The Morita centre of perfect modules over an algebroid is the derived global section complex of its
Hochschild cochains. Apply the globalized formality equivalence and then derived global sections. A K3 surface is
holomorphic symplectic, so the stated Hamiltonian sector is the resulting Poisson-BV complex.

58.1   The algebraic mixed-HT realization
The sheaf
                                           P𝑆 = (PV 𝑆 ⟦ℏ⟧, [ℏ 𝜋, −])
is a sheaf of complete homotopy-E2 algebras. Dunn additivity gives its locally constant factorization
realization in two oriented topological directions. The holomorphic sheaf variable retains the dependence
on 𝑆 .

Theorem 58.1 (Algebraic factorization realization). The homotopy-E2 sheaf P𝑆 determines a complete
mixed holomorphic–topological factorization algebra
                                             alg
                                           A 𝑆,bulk   on   ℝ2top × 𝑆.

Its universal boundary category is Perf (W𝑆,ℏ ), and the bulk–boundary morphism is a local equivalence
                                        alg    ∼   der
                                                       Perf (W𝑆,ℏ ) .
                                                                   
                                      A 𝑆,bulk −
                                               → 𝑍 Mor

On every formal Darboux chart the map is the completed Kontsevich–Duﬂo equivalence.
Proof. The factorization envelope of a complete homotopy-E2 algebra is locally constant on oriented two-disks.
Sheafwise application to P𝑆 gives the first assertion. The Fedosov algebroid is the boundary quantization of the
same linear Poisson data. Globalized formality identifies its Hochschild cochains with P𝑆 , and Morita invariance
identifies Hochschild cochains with the centre of the perfect-module category. The construction is local on 𝑆 and
therefore respects factorization descent.

The cyclic Lie sheaf of formal Hamiltonians has the shifted-cotangent action

The finite jet zero modes carry the exact quantum measure constructed above.

Thus the 𝑁 -brane stack is a representation of the universal boundary algebra.
Proof. For a polynomial Hamiltonian 𝑓 , cyclic differentiation gives the matrix vector field
                                             cyc                                      cyc
                              𝛿 𝑓 ( 𝑋 ) = − 𝜕𝑌 𝑓 ( 𝑋 , 𝑌 ) ,           𝛿 𝑓 (𝑌 ) = 𝜕𝑋 𝑓 ( 𝑋 , 𝑌 ) .

first for polynomial Hamiltonians and then continuously for the formal completion. This proves that 𝜌 𝑁 is a

which is the Rees relation. The universal property of the completed Rees enveloping algebra gives the displayed
morphism. It is the universal boundary action evaluated in the 𝑁 -dimensional Chan–Paton representation.

are connected by explicit maps. The first two are equivalent by formality. The stable trace map reaches

Their common input is the cyclic Hamiltonian class, and each construction retains a different amount

Theorem 65.1 (Four realizations of cyclic primitives). For a unital algebra 𝐵 and a Hamiltonian Lie
map 𝔥 → 𝐻 𝐶0 ( 𝐵 ), the following maps are functorial:

                                    Sym

The ﬁrst map preserves the homotopy Gerstenhaber structure, the second preserves the necklace Poisson bracket,
the third preserves multiplication, and the fourth preserves the Hopf structure.
Proof. The first arrow is the inverse limit of the finite-jet Kontsevich–Duflo formality maps. The graph formulas

on the representation scheme. Cyclicity makes this function independent of the representative of [ 𝑏 ]. The double-
Poisson or Hamiltonian hypothesis on 𝔥 → 𝐻 𝐶0 ( 𝐵 ) converts the necklace bracket into the Poisson bracket of
trace functions, which proves the second assertion.
    Products of single traces define compatible multisymmetric functions as 𝑁 grows. The universal property of
the completed symmetric algebra therefore gives the third map. Its multiplicativity is built into the passage from a
cyclic primitive to its multi-trace exponential assembly.
    The stable Lie algebra 𝔤𝔩 ∞ 𝐵 has block-sum coproduct. The Loday–Quillen–Tsygan theorem identifies its
primitive homology with 𝐻 𝐶∗−1 ( 𝐵 ) and identifies the full stable homology with the free graded-commutative

Hopf algebra on those primitives. This gives the fourth isomorphism and its Hopf compatibility. Naturality of
formality, trace evaluation, stabilization, and the Loday–Quillen–Tsygan map proves functoriality of the four
constructions.

                                                                                  Synthesis
The finite jets supply exact BV theories. Their boundary quantizations supply universal branes. Linear
Poisson formality identifies bulk and centre. Universal graph formulas pass to the formal limit. Fedosov
descent globalizes the equivalence. Matrix substitution produces finite Chan–Paton branes, necklace
Hamiltonians, and stable traces. The Duflo wheels measure the quantum difference between symmetric
and enveloping coordinates.
   The complete chain is

A direct Jacobi check for Hamiltonian jets
For monomials
                                         𝑓 = 𝑧1𝑎 𝑧2𝑏 ,       𝑔 = 𝑧1𝑐 𝑧2𝑑 ,
one has
                                    { 𝑓 , 𝑔 } = ( 𝑎𝑑 − 𝑏𝑐 ) 𝑧1𝑎+𝑐 −1 𝑧2𝑏+𝑑 −1 .
Substitution of three monomials gives a scalar multiple of

                    det(( 𝑎, 𝑏 ) , ( 𝑐, 𝑑 )) det(( 𝑎 + 𝑐 − 1, 𝑏 + 𝑑 − 1) , ( 𝑒, 𝑓 )) + cyclic.

Writing the three coefficients in terms of the 2 × 2 determinants of the exponent vectors shows pairwise
cancellation. The coordinate-free proof is [ 𝜋, 𝜋 ] Sch = 0 for 𝜋 = 𝜕𝑧1 ∧ 𝜕𝑧2 .

                                                          Bibliographic note
The finite and global formality maps used above are the linear and covariant forms of Kontsevich formality
and its Fedosov globalization [14, 27, 53]. The Hochschild model of enveloping algebras is the Cartan–
Eilenberg comparison. The all-arity centre action is the Deligne–Tamarkin brace action [54, 61]. The
global deformation-quantization category is the holomorphic Weyl algebroid. The matrix trace and
necklace formulas are the noncommutative symplectic moment-map construction.

                                                       Δ5𝜃 = 26 𝐷5 .

Divisor, character, and the meaning of the
                               square root
The diagonal Humbert divisor is
                                     𝐻1 = { 𝑍 ∈ ℍ2 : 𝑧 = 0}/Sp4 (ℤ) .
Theorem 72.1 (Diagonal divisor). The divisor of 𝐷5 is the Sp4 (ℤ)-orbit of 𝐻1 , with multiplicity one. The
form transforms by the Maass character 𝜈5 of order two, and 𝜒10 = 𝐷5 2 is scalar-valued.
Proof. This is the defining diagonal-divisor property of the Igusa square root and the first row of the Cléry–
Gritsenko classification [17, 47, 48].

    Real roots of the denominator algebra define Weyl reflections. Imaginary roots contribute root
spaces and product exponents. This division of labor is intrinsic to a Borcherds algebra: the Weyl group
is generated by real simple roots, while the imaginary simple roots enter the correction term of the
denominator identity.

72.1    Automorphic section, orientation line, and Pfaffian operator
An automorphic square root is a section of a line bundle with character. An orientation is a square root
of a determinant line. A Pfaffian operator is a skew family whose regularized Pfaffian is a section of that
orientation line. These form a sequence of successive refinements:
                        automorphic section −→ determinant-line identification
                −→ oriented square-root line −→ Fredholm family and Pfaffian section.
Proposition 72.2 (Logical separation of the four levels). The product theorem constructs the ﬁrst object
in the sequence. A determinant-line comparison constructs the second. A coherent square root of that line
constructs the third. A ﬁrst-order operator requires a symbol, domain, ellipticity or Fredholm condition,
and regularization, and thereby produces the fourth reﬁnement.
Proof. Each stage adds a specified geometric structure. A determinant realization adds an isomorphism from a
geometric determinant line to the automorphic line. An oriented realization adds a coherent square-root line and
its parity refinement. An operator realization adds a principal symbol, a Fredholm domain, a spectrum, and a
regularization whose Pfaffian is the resulting section.

   The title names the monic automorphic section 𝐷5 , characterized by the exact identity 𝐷5 2 = 𝜒10 .
Part IV formulates an analytic Pfaffian realization as a further geometric refinement.

  The Gritsenko–Nikulin Lie superalgebra
The Borcherds product determines a generalized Kac–Moody Lie superalgebra 𝔤Δ5 with real simple
roots 𝛿1 , 𝛿2 , 𝛿3 and an imaginary correction system determined by 𝜙0,1 .

Theorem 74.1 (Root supermultiplicities and denominator). On the active positive support,

                                       smult 𝔤Δ5 𝛼 ( 𝑛,ℓ ,𝑚 ) = 𝑓 ( 𝑛𝑚, ℓ ) .

In the Lorentzian coordinate convention of Gritsenko–Nikulin,

                                   den(𝔤Δ5 ) = 𝐷5 (2 𝑍 ) = 2−6 Δ5𝜃 (2 𝑍 ) .

Proof. The real roots and Weyl chamber are the type-II case of the Gritsenko–Nikulin construction. The imaginary
simple-root correction is chosen so that its Weyl–Kac–Borcherds product has exponent 𝑓 ( 𝑛𝑚, ℓ ). The resulting
product is the doubled-variable monic Igusa form [41–43].

   The theorem gives a Lie superalgebra with a bracket, Cartan, Weyl group, root parity, and denomina-
tor. The scalar product alone records the signed dimensions of the root spaces; the Gritsenko–Nikulin
construction supplies the algebraic operations.

Corollary 75.2 (Bar denominator identity). On the formal root torus,

                                    𝑒 − 𝜌 𝜒root 𝐶∗∧,ℎ (𝔫Δ5 ,+ ) = 𝐷5 ( 𝑍 root ) .

in Siegel period coordinates.
Proof. Multiply the Euler product in the preceding theorem by the Weyl monomial 𝑒 − 𝜌 . The denominator
theorem identifies the result on the root torus, and the stated embedding gives the Siegel-coordinate formula.

   The denominator is therefore the Weyl-shifted Euler supercharacter of the height-complete interval
bar of the positive enveloping algebra.

Theorem 76.2 (Finite-height BF realization). The action 𝑆 𝐻 satisﬁes the classical master equation and
deﬁnes a classical holomorphic–topological factorization algebra. Harmonic reduction along a compact
interval produces the ﬁnite-dimensional cotangent BV theory of 𝔫Δ5 ,+ ( 𝐻 ) . The positive-root height grading
makes every even adjoint endomorphism strictly height-increasing and every odd adjoint endomorphism
odd; hence the modular supercharacter vanishes and the reduced theory satisﬁes the quantum master equa-
tion.
    Choose the standard complementary Lagrangian boundary polarizations, ﬁxing the 𝔫Δ5 ,+ ( 𝐻 ) [1] coor-

Proof. The classical master equation is the Jacobi identity paired with invariance of the cotangent pairing. Elliptic
descent gives the classical factorization algebra. Harmonic reduction along the interval gives a finite-dimensional
BV space. If 𝑥 is even of positive root height, ad 𝑥 raises height and is nilpotent on the finite quotient, so its
restrictions to the even and odd summands both have trace zero; if 𝑥 is odd, ad 𝑥 is odd and has supertrace zero.
Thus the modular character vanishes and the finite BV quantum master equation follows.
    The chosen boundary polarization quantizes the linear Kirillov–Kostant boundary bracket to 𝑈 (𝔫Δ5 ,+ ( 𝐻 ) ).

                            Point-local and wrapped sectors
Let 𝑝 𝐸 : 𝑆 × 𝐸 → 𝐸 be projection.

Lemma 79.1 (Positive elliptic degree is wrapped). Let 𝐶 ⊂ 𝑆 × 𝐸 be a proper one-dimensional subscheme
whose pushforward ( 𝑝 𝐸 )∗ [𝐶 ] has positive degree. Then 𝑝 𝐸 ( 𝐶 ) = 𝐸 .
Proof. Properness makes 𝑝 𝐸 ( 𝐶 ) closed. Positive pushforward degree implies that at least one irreducible compo-
nent of 𝐶 dominates a one-dimensional closed subset of 𝐸 . Every one-dimensional closed subset of the irreducible
curve 𝐸 is 𝐸 itself, so the image of that component, and hence the image of 𝐶 , is all of 𝐸 .

   Projection-finite objects define point-supported sectors on Ran( 𝐸 ). Positive elliptic degree defines
wrapped sectors supported over the entire elliptic curve.

Definition 79.2 (The local–wrapped factorization operad). Let Ch 𝐸 denote the chosen chi-
ral/factorization operad on 𝐸 and let Ass denote the ordered associative operad. Define the colored
operad Hyb 𝐸 with colors
                                       l,    w𝑚 ( 𝑚 ≥ 1)

by the following operation objects.
   (i) Operations with local output are

                                                 Hyb 𝐸 ( l ⊗ 𝑘 ; l) = Ch 𝐸 ( 𝑘 ) .

  (ii) For 𝑟 ≥ 1 and 𝑚 = 𝑚1 + · · · + 𝑚𝑟 , operations with wrapped output are

                                Hyb 𝐸 ( l ⊗ 𝑘 , w𝑚1 , . . . , w𝑚𝑟 ; w𝑚 ) = Ch 𝐸 ( 𝑘 ) ⊗ Ass ( 𝑟 ) .

       All other operation objects are initial.
Operadic composition uses chiral insertion and disjoint union in the first factor and ordered substitution
in the second. The equality

                                ( 𝛾1 ◦ 𝛾2 ) ⊗ ( 𝑎1 ◦ 𝑎2 ) = ( 𝛾1 ⊗ 𝑎1 ) ◦ ( 𝛾2 ⊗ 𝑎2 )

is the mixed interchange law. The arities ( 𝑘, 𝑟 ) = ( 𝑘, 0) , (0, 2) , ( 𝑘, 1) recover, respectively, local factor-
ization, wrapped multiplication, and the action of local insertions on a wrapped sector.

                      The formal Igusa quantum group
                                                                                             ∨,gr
Let 𝔟+ be the positive Borcherds Borel half. Form the restricted graded dual 𝔟− := 𝔟+ with bracket
and cobracket transported by the invariant pairing, identify the two Cartan copies along their common
radical quotient, and complete by a proper root height. The completed Drinfeld double of this paired
Borel datum is denoted 𝔤Δ5 pair . Its positive and negative halves, diagonal Cartan, invariant form, and
canonical tensor form a height-admissible Manin triple and hence a quasitriangular Lie superbialgebra.

Definition 81.1 (Height-admissible Lie superbialgebra). A root-graded Lie superbialgebra is height-
admissible when every root space is finite-dimensional, every bounded-height region contains finitely
many roots, and the bracket, cobracket, invariant tensor, and all iterated operations are coefficientwise
finite in the height completion.

Proposition 81.2 (Height admissibility). The paired Gritsenko–Nikulin Borcherds Lie superbialgebra
𝔤Δ5 pair is height-admissible.
Proof. The Borcherds root spaces are finite-dimensional and the chosen height is proper. The bracket and co-
bracket preserve total root degree. In a bounded total height, finitely many decompositions of a root occur. The
canonical tensor is therefore a coefficientwise finite sum in every height quotient.

Theorem 81.3 (Quantized Borcherds superalgebra). Choose the symmetrizable Borcherds–Cartan super-
datum and multiplicity index set that present 𝔤Δ5 . Hong’s construction gives a quantized enveloping Hopf
superalgebra
                                               𝑈𝑞 (𝔤Δ5 )
over ℚ( 𝑞 ), generated by its Cartan elements and the real and imaginary simple-root generators, with
triangular decomposition

                              𝑈𝑞 (𝔤Δ5 )  𝑈𝑞− (𝔤Δ5 ) ⊗ 𝑈𝑞0 (𝔤Δ5 ) ⊗ 𝑈𝑞+ (𝔤Δ5 ) .

Its PBW root spaces have the same graded ranks as the classical Borcherds superalgebra. The canonical
pairing of the two Borel halves yields a universal 𝑅-matrix in the root-height completion of

                                           𝑈𝑞+ (𝔤Δ5 ) ⊗
                                                      b 𝑈𝑞− (𝔤Δ5 ) .

Consequently the quantization preserves the root-multiplicity data that enter 𝐷5 (2 𝑍 ).




                                                                                                            217
81. The formal Igusa quantum group                                                                             218


Proof. The Gritsenko–Nikulin algebra is a symmetrizable Borcherds–Kac–Moody Lie superalgebra. Apply
the generators-and-relations construction of the quantized Borcherds superalgebra. The nondegenerate bilinear
form, triangular decomposition, centre, and universal 𝑅-matrix are established by Hong [46]. The PBW theorem
identifies the associated graded root spaces with their classical counterparts. Since the denominator exponents are
the classical signed root multiplicities, they are unchanged by the flat root-graded deformation.

Remark 81.4. The quantum group is attached to the Borcherds generalized Cartan superdatum. A
geometric Hall quantum group is identified with it through a Hall coproduct, pairing, completion, and
primitive comparison.

Theorem 82.1 (Classical local and exact zero-mode realization). For every 𝐻 :
   (i) 𝑆 𝐻 satisﬁes the classical master equation and deﬁnes a classical holomorphic–topological factoriza-
       tion algebra;
  (ii) harmonic reduction along a compact interval gives the ﬁnite-dimensional cotangent BV theory of
       𝔫Δ5 ,+ ( 𝐻 ) , whose positive-root grading makes its modular supercharacter zero and hence gives the
       quantum master equation;
 (iii) after choosing the complementary Lagrangian boundary polarizations, the boundary algebra is
       𝑈 (𝔫Δ5 ,+ ( 𝐻 ) ), the interval amplitude is 1 ⊗𝑈𝕃 (𝔫 ( 𝐻 ) ) 1 ≃ 𝐶∗ (𝔫Δ5 ,+ ( 𝐻 ) ), and the holomorphic
                                                             Δ5 , +
       boundary current envelope is 𝑉𝐻 .
                                                             bℎ (𝔫Δ ,+ ), interval complex 𝐶 ∧ (𝔫Δ ,+ ),
The transition maps yield a pro-object with boundary algebra 𝑈     5                        ∗,ℎ   5

and current envelope 𝑉
                     bΔ .
                          5


Proof. The classical master equation is the Jacobi identity and invariance of the cotangent pairing. The classical
observables satisfy factorization descent. Harmonic reduction is finite-dimensional. An even adjoint operator raises
positive-root height and is nilpotent on the finite quotient; an odd adjoint operator is odd. Both have supertrace
zero, so the modular term in the finite BV Laplacian vanishes. The chosen polarization quantizes the boundary
Lie–Poisson bracket to the enveloping algebra; the two augmentation states give the two-sided bar, identified
with Chevalley chains by PBW. Holomorphic descendants give the current algebra. All structure maps preserve
height.

   The theorem gives the precise field-theoretic realization used in this volume: a classical local theory
together with an exact quantum protected zero-mode sector.

Proof. The preceding convergence theorem gives the trace-class criterion for the diagonal operator. The Fredholm

the Gritsenko–Nikulin denominator formula.

Remark 83.3. The polarization and orientation in this theorem belong to the root Hilbert space. A
compact moduli-space orientation is a separate line bundle and is compared with this one by a specified
intertwiner.
    This theorem constructs the square-root operator on the universal Borcherds root Hilbert superspace.
A geometric realization is a determinant-line intertwiner from a protected geometric Hilbert complex
to this universal operator family.

                                                                                           CHAPTER
                                                                                                                    84

                        What the automorphic character
                                           determines
Theorem 84.1 (Denominator data and reconstruction data). The Borcherds product determines:
   (i) the signed root multiplicity sdim 𝔤Δ5 𝛼 in every active root degree;
  (ii) the Weyl vector and denominator character;
 (iii) the divisor and automorphic multiplier of the section.
A Lie bracket is supplied by the Borcherds–Kac–Moody presentation, parity dimensions by a parity-reﬁned
root datum, Hall multiplication by extension correspondences, and an oriented square root by a square-root
line with monodromy. These reconstruction data reﬁne the scalar product independently.
Proof. The first three items are read from the exponent, prefactor, and automorphy law. Independence is witnessed
by explicit pairs: two brackets on the same graded vector space have the same PBW character; an even–odd pair has
signed dimension zero; distinct Hall correspondences may have the same Euler product; and twisting a square-root
line by a sign local system preserves its square. Hence each refined object is reconstructed from the additional
datum named in the statement.

Recognition Theorem 84.2 (Complete primitive recognition). Let 𝔭 be a positive graded Lie superalge-
bra and let Ψ : 𝔫Δ5 ,+ ↠ 𝔭 be a graded Lie map. The map is an isomorphism if either:
   (i) the parity-reﬁned Hilbert series of 𝑈 (𝔫Δ5 ,+ ) and 𝑈 (𝔭) agree; or
  (ii) each primitive degree has prescribed pure parity and the signed PBW characters agree.
The induced height completions, Chevalley complexes, and current envelopes are then isomorphic.
Proof. Choose the first height containing a homogeneous kernel vector. All decomposable PBW terms in that
height are determined by lower heights and agree. The primitive parity dimension therefore drops, contradicting
the first hypothesis. In the second case the logarithm of the PBW product recovers signed primitive dimensions
inductively, and prescribed parity recovers ordinary dimensions. Naturality of completion, Chevalley chains, and
current envelopes gives the final assertion.

                                       Finite simple-root windows
Let 𝐼Δ be the multiset of simple roots of the Gritsenko–Nikulin Borcherds superalgebra. A repeated
imaginary simple root is written once for each copy. The parity map is

                                                 𝑝 : 𝐼Δ −→ ℤ/2.

Choose a proper height function
                                                 ℎ : 𝐼Δ −→ ℤ>0
whose sublevel sets are finite. Put

                                         𝐼𝐻 = { 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼Δ : ℎ ( 𝑖 ) ≤ 𝐻 } .

The restriction of the invariant form gives a finite symmetric Borcherds–Cartan matrix

                                   𝐴 𝐻 = ( 𝑎𝑖𝑗 ) 𝑖,𝑗 ∈ 𝐼𝐻 ,

The evenness of the root lattice makes every imaginary diagonal entry even.

                                            ⟨𝑒 𝑖 , 𝑒𝑗 ⟩ + ⟨𝑒𝑗 , 𝑒 𝑖 ⟩ = 𝑎𝑖𝑗 .

           Hall realization of the positive algebra

Theorem 87.1 (Finite-window Hall theorem). The composition subalgebra generated by the simple repre-
sentations of 𝑄 Δ,𝐻 is the positive quantum generalized Kac–Moody algebra attached to the underlying
even Borcherds–Cartan matrix 𝐴 𝐻 . Semisimple perverse sheaves on the representation varieties give its
canonical basis. The semi-derived Hall algebra of two-periodic complexes realizes the corresponding full
ordinary quantum generalized Kac–Moody algebra after Cartan reduction.
Proof. The positive statement is the quiver-with-loops construction of Kang–Schiffmann. Their perverse-sheaf
category gives the canonical basis. Lu’s semi-derived Hall theorem realizes the full quantum generalized Kac–
Moody algebra from the same quiver with loops; the Cartan reduction identifies the two torus copies [3,50,59].

87.1     Parity and the quantized superdatum
The Gritsenko–Nikulin denominator algebra carries a parity map on its simple-root multiset. Extend
it additively to the root lattice. Hong’s construction applied to this Borcherds superdatum gives the
positive Borel
                                              𝑈𝑞+ (𝔤Δ5 ( 𝐻 ) )
and the full quantized Borcherds superalgebra 𝑈𝑞 (𝔤Δ5 ( 𝐻 ) ).
Theorem 87.2 (Parallel Hall and supergroup realizations). The quiver Hall algebra and Hong’s quantum
supergroup are two functorial realizations of the same ﬁnite Borcherds–Cartan window:
   (i) the quiver Hall algebra realizes the underlying ordinary generalized Kac–Moody presentation and
       its canonical basis;
  (ii) Hong’s algebra realizes the parity-reﬁned super presentation and its universal 𝑅-matrix;
 (iii) their root lattices, Cartan forms, height ﬁltrations, and Weyl denominators agree after applying the
       parity supertrace.
Proof. The first statement is the finite-window Hall theorem. The second is Hong’s quantized Borcherds-
superalgebra theorem. Both presentations use 𝐴 𝐻 and the same simple-root lattice. The parity map changes
ordinary dimensions into superdimensions in the PBW character. The denominator theorem identifies this signed
PBW product with the Gritsenko–Nikulin product.

                     The three-Calabi–Yau completion
Let 𝑘𝑄 Δ,𝐻 be the path algebra. Its completed three-Calabi–Yau completion is

                                             ΓΔ,𝐻 = Π3 ( 𝑘𝑄Δ,𝐻 ) .

Equivalently, it is the Ginzburg dg algebra of the doubled quiver with a degree −1 reverse arrow for every
arrow and a degree −2 loop at every vertex, with differential determined by the canonical potential.

Theorem 88.1 (Borcherds–Ginzburg tower). For every 𝐻 :
   (i) ΓΔ,𝐻 is a complete three-Calabi–Yau dg algebra;
  (ii) the degree-zero inclusion 𝑘𝑄 Δ,𝐻 → ΓΔ,𝐻 is functorial in full-subquiver inclusions;
 (iii) the Hall composition algebra of 𝑘𝑄Δ,𝐻 is the underlying ordinary positive quantum generalized
       Kac–Moody algebra;
 (iv) the inclusions 𝐻 ≤ 𝐻 ′ form a compatible tower of dg categories and Hall algebras.
Proof. The Calabi–Yau assertion is Keller’s theorem on deformed Calabi–Yau completions, in the Ginzburg
model [35, 51]. Full-subquiver inclusions extend to reverse arrows, loops, and differentials. The Hall statement is
the finite-window Hall theorem.

   Define the proper-height objects

                             ΓΔ = lim ΓΔ,𝐻 ,         b𝑞 (𝔤Δ ) = lim 𝑈𝑞 (𝔤Δ ( 𝐻 ) ) .

                                     𝐻                             𝐻

The first is locally three-Calabi–Yau: every finite collection of objects lies in a finite three-Calabi–Yau
window. The second is Hong’s root-height-complete quantum symmetry.

                                                                                             CHAPTER
                                                                                                                       90
    Coordinate-framed orientation on finite
                             critical charts
Fix 𝐻 and a dimension vector d. The representation space of the graded doubled quiver underlying ΓΔ,𝐻
is affine. Order its vertex, arrow, reverse-arrow, and degree-2 loop coordinates. The corresponding volume
line is trivial. The derived moment-map equations form a symmetric three-Calabi–Yau deformation
complex whose determinant is the square of this ordered coordinate line.

Theorem 90.1 (Multiplicative coordinate-framed orientation). A choice of order for the vertex, arrow,
reverse-arrow, and loop coordinates trivializes the ambient canonical line and supplies an orientation of
every ﬁnite-dimensional critical representation chart of ΓΔ,𝐻 . Under direct sum and the Hall extension cor-
respondence, the orientation line is the tensor product of the two diagonal orientation lines. The construction
is associative for three summands and compatible with full-subquiver inclusions.
Proof. On the extension chart, every arrow matrix is block upper triangular. The tangent complex splits into two
diagonal deformation complexes and two off-diagonal complexes exchanged by three-Calabi–Yau duality. The
paired off-diagonal part carries the standard determinant square root induced by the three-Calabi–Yau duality
pairing. The ordered diagonal coordinate lines therefore multiply. A three-block decomposition gives the same
tensor order in both parenthesizations. Full-subquiver inclusion appends an ordered set of coordinates.

Corollary 90.2 (Oriented critical chart tower). The ﬁnite critical charts carry compatible coordinate-
framed vanishing-cycle complexes. Their Grothendieck groups reﬁne the quiver Hall tower by the three-
Calabi–Yau critical coeﬃcients.
Proof. The chosen coordinate framings are compatible with direct sum, extension correspondences, and full-
subquiver inclusions in the fixed root order. Vanishing cycles are functorial for these oriented critical charts, so
their convolution complexes and Grothendieck groups form the stated tower.

Theorem 91.2 (Local–wrapped factorization). FΔ,𝐸 is a graded factorization coalgebra on Ranwr ( 𝐸 ). Its

Theorem 93.1 (Classical local theory and exact quantum harmonic sector). For every ﬁnite height 𝐻
the shifted-cotangent action deﬁnes a classical mixed holomorphic–topological factorization algebra on
ℝ2top × 𝐸 . Harmonic reduction on a compact product cell gives the ﬁnite-dimensional cotangent BV theory
of 𝔫Δ5 ,+ ( 𝐻 ) , and this reduced theory satisﬁes the quantum master equation. Complementary Lagrangian
boundary polarizations give the boundary enveloping algebra 𝑈 (𝔫Δ5 ,+ ( 𝐻 ) ), the two-sided bar complex
𝐶∗ (𝔫Δ5 ,+ ( 𝐻 ) ), and the current envelope on 𝐸 . The proper-height inverse system is coeﬃcientwise ﬁnite and
deﬁnes a complete classical local theory together with an exact quantum harmonic sector.
Proof. The invariant cotangent pairing and the Jacobi identity give the classical master equation and factorization
descent. Harmonic reduction leaves a finite positive nilpotent Lie superalgebra. Every even adjoint endomorphism
raises proper height and is strictly upper triangular; every odd adjoint endomorphism has vanishing supertrace. The
reduced BV divergence therefore vanishes. Canonical quantization in the selected polarization gives the enveloping
algebra, and the augmentation boundary states give the two-sided bar, identified with Chevalley chains by PBW.
Each map preserves height, so the inverse system is defined coefficientwise.

     Absolute convergence of the virtual root
                                    operator
For each positive root 𝛼 , let 𝑉𝛼 be the minimal parity realization of the signed multiplicity: it is purely
even of dimension sdim 𝔤Δ5 𝛼 when this integer is nonnegative and purely odd of dimension − sdim 𝔤Δ5 𝛼
when it is negative. Set
                                                  M
                                            V = c 𝑉 .   Δ        𝛼 >0 𝛼
This virtual root superspace is the canonical linear carrier of the denominator character; the Lie bracket
remains carried by 𝔤Δ5 .
   Let ℎ ( 𝛼 ) be the proper height. Jacobi-form coefficient estimates give constants 𝐶0 , 𝐶1 > 0 such that

                                    dim 𝑉𝛼 = sdim 𝔤Δ5 𝛼 ≤ 𝐶0 exp( 𝐶1 ℎ ( 𝛼 ))

for every positive root. The number of Gram triples of height 𝐻 is polynomial in 𝐻 .

For suﬃciently large 𝑡 > 0,
                                     dim 𝑉𝛼 𝑒 − 𝛼 ( 𝑡 𝑍 0 ) < ∞.

Proof. Interior positivity gives a constant 𝑐 ( 𝑍 0 ) > 0 with ℜ 𝛼 ( 𝑍 0 ) ≥ 𝑐 ( 𝑍 0 ) ℎ ( 𝛼 ). Combine this with (94). The

Corollary 94.2 (Trace-class root Hamiltonian). On the conical tail, the diagonal operator

Proof. The trace norm is the summable series of the preceding theorem, separated by parity. Standard trace-class

95.1    Automorphic orientation
The product defines a section of the automorphic line L 5 ⊗ 𝜈5 . On the absolute-convergence chamber,
the oriented Fredholm Pfaffian is a trivialization of this line. Analytic continuation uses the automorphy
factor of 𝐷5 and therefore glues the chamberwise Fredholm models with transition character 𝜈5 .

Corollary 95.2 (Orientation character). The automorphic orientation line of the root Dirac family is the
sign local system 𝜈5 . On every real simple reﬂection it has value −1.


                                                                                                             247
95. The root Dirac operator                                                                                       248


Proof. The Fredholm product equals 𝐷5 on the convergence chamber, and 𝐷5 transforms with automorphy
factor 𝜈5 . This transformation law is the gluing rule for the automorphic orientation line. A real simple root occurs
with multiplicity one, so reflection reverses its oriented normal line.

Definition 96.1 (Universal Igusa completion). The protected completion selected by the K3 half-index
is the quintuple
                                                              hyb

                                      𝔎Δ = ΓΔ , BΔ , H1p , FΔ,𝐸 , 𝔸 𝑍 .

Its components are the locally three-Calabi–Yau completion, the ordinary quiver Hall category, the
Borcherds one-particle Lie superalgebra, the local–wrapped current factorization algebra, and the virtual
root Dirac family.

Theorem 96.2 (Igusa protected completion). The quintuple 𝔎Δ satisﬁes:
   (i) its one-particle Lie superalgebra is 𝔤Δ5 ;
  (ii) its ordinary Hall presentation and Hong’s parity-reﬁned quantum supergroup share the Borcherds–
       Cartan grading and signed PBW denominator;
 (iii) its chiral primitive algebra on 𝐸 is the local–wrapped current algebra of 𝔤Δ5 ;
 (iv) its raw multiparticle supercharacter is 𝑃 −1 ,where 𝑒 − 𝜌 𝑃 = DenΔ ; equivalently 𝑒 𝜌 sch Fock(𝔫Δ5 ,+ ) =
      DenΔ−1 ;
  (v) the half-coordinate pullback of the doubled Weyl-normalized character is − 𝜒10 −1 in the Oberdieck–
      Pixton normalization;
 (vi) its oriented ﬁrst-order determinant is DenΔ and its orientation monodromy is 𝜈5 .
Proof. The first three statements are the Borcherds root, quiver Hall, Hong quantum-supergroup, Ginzburg, and
colored-Ran theorems. PBW gives the fourth. The identity 𝜒10 = 𝐷5 2 and the established reduced scalar theorem
give the fifth. The Fredholm theorem gives the sixth.

    This theorem constructs the protected effective theory selected by the Igusa denominator. A geomet-
ric type-IIA or topological-string model determines a realization functor into this universal completion
by its primitive states and extension amplitudes. Equivalence of protected observables is equivalence of
the oriented Hall and factorization objects carried by that functor.

                                                                                                                    97
                                                             Universal property
A presentation-preserving protected realization consists of an oriented root-graded theory 𝔗, a map
from each Igusa simple-root space to its primitive states, and chosen comparison maps from the Hall,
Ginzburg, current, quantum-double, and determinant functors applied to that simple-root map.

Theorem 97.1 (Universal presentation map). Every presentation-preserving protected realization carries
a unique Lie-superalgebra morphism

                                             𝔫Δ5 ,+ −→ Prim(𝔗)

extending the simple-root map. Functoriality produces compatible morphisms from the Hall algebra, the
Ginzburg completion, the local–wrapped current theory, the completed quantum group, and the virtual
root determinant of 𝔎Δ to their selected targets in 𝔗. These morphisms are equivalences when the primitive
map and the selected functorial comparison maps are equivalences.
Proof. The Borcherds generators and relations give the unique primitive Lie morphism. Hall convolution, Cal-
abi–Yau completion, chiral Chevalley chains, quantized doubling, and the diagonal Fredholm construction are
functorial in the root presentation. PBW promotes a primitive equivalence to the completed enveloping and Fock
objects. The remaining equivalences are exactly the selected comparison maps in the definition of a presentation-
preserving realization.

                        The eight diagonal-divisor forms
Cléry and Gritsenko classify eight genus-two modular forms whose divisor is the diagonal with order
one [17]. Gritsenko–Nikulin and the CHL denominator constructions give the sourced reflective pre-
sentations used for the corresponding rows [37–39, 41–43]. Every row has a minimal virtual product
superspace obtained from its Fourier exponents, together with a Fock determinant and a diagonal Fred-
holm operator. A sourced denominator presentation refines this product model by its Borcherds–Cartan
bracket, Weyl group, and quantum supergroup. The half-integral rows are formed on their multiplier
covers.

Theorem 98.1 (Eight-row algebraic realization). Each diagonal-divisor row has a proper-height virtual
root superspace, a super-Fock determinant, a colored abelian current theory, and a trace-class Fredholm
denominator operator whose character is the corresponding automorphic product. For every row equipped
with its reﬂective denominator presentation, the ﬁnite-window Borcherds quiver, ordinary Hall category,
three-Calabi–Yau completion, and parity-reﬁned quantum supergroup reﬁne this product model and
recover the same denominator character.
Proof. Local finiteness of the product exponents gives the graded superspace and its proper-height completion.
The Fock and Fredholm constructions depend only on these signed multiplicities and reproduce the product
coefficientwise. A reflective denominator presentation supplies a symmetrizable Borcherds superdatum; the quiver,
Hall, Ginzburg, current, and Hong constructions then apply to that datum and preserve its PBW denominator.

                                                                                                 261
                                                                                    CHAPTER
                                                                                                             102
   Concordance with the earlier Igusa draft
The reconstitution retains the half-index determinant, theta normalization, Borcherds denominator,
reduced scalar theorem, charge Gram map, and geometric Hall comparison datum. It reorganizes their
dependencies as follows:
  (i) the automorphic square root is completed entirely within the Borcherds product theorem;
  (ii) the BKM bracket comes from the Gritsenko–Nikulin algebra;
 (iii) the bar–Chevalley comparison gives the denominator a homological source;
 (iv) the current and BF constructions give an exact first-order algebraic model;
  (v) orientation data refine determinant lines, while an operator Pfaffian requires an actual Fredholm
      family;
 (vi) Weyl reflections are attached to real roots, and imaginary roots enter through multiplicities;
 (vii) the compact Hall comparison uses additive Mukai charge, the polarized central extension, and
       hybrid local–wrapped sectors.

                                                     Calabi–Yau categories
103.1    Smoothness, properness, and the Calabi–Yau class
Let C be a small stable 𝕜-linear category. Smoothness means that the diagonal bimodule is perfect.
Properness means that the morphism complexes are perfect over 𝕜. A 𝑑 -Calabi–Yau structure is a
nondegenerate negative-cyclic class
                                          𝜃 CY ∈ 𝐻 𝐶𝑑− (C)
whose underlying Hochschild trace identifies the diagonal bimodule with its dual shifted by 𝑑 .
  For 𝐸 ∈ C, the tangent complex of the derived moduli stack of objects is

                                       𝑇𝐸 M C ≃ RHom C ( 𝐸, 𝐸 ) [1] .

The Calabi–Yau trace pairs two tangent vectors by composition and trace.

Theorem 103.1 (The two universal categorical outputs). Let C be a smooth proper 𝑑 -Calabi–Yau category.
   (i) The Hochschild cochains 𝐶𝐶 ∗ (C) carry a framed E2 -algebra structure.
  (ii) Whenever the moduli stack of objects is representable in the PTVV setting, M C carries a (2 − 𝑑 )-
       shifted symplectic form.
Proof. The cyclic Deligne theorem upgrades the brace E2 structure on Hochschild cochains by the circle action
supplied by the cyclic trace [11]. The shifted form at 𝐸 is

                                                ◦                      Tr
                          RHom( 𝐸, 𝐸 ) [1] ⊗2 −−→ RHom( 𝐸, 𝐸 ) [2] −−→ 𝕜 [2 − 𝑑 ] .

Nondegeneracy is the Calabi–Yau duality isomorphism, and closedness is the negative-cyclic lift [10, 66].

  The integer 𝑑 controls the shift of the moduli form. The Hochschild operation remains framed E2 .
These structures meet through deformation theory while retaining their separate operadic degrees.

                 Local BV theories and factorization
                                         observables
104.1    Cyclic elliptic local Lie algebras
Let 𝑋 be a complex manifold. A classical local field theory is specified by a sheaf L of elliptic 𝐿∞ algebras,
a local invariant pairing, and an action functional 𝑆 satisfying the classical master equation. For an open
set 𝑈 ⊂ 𝑋 , the classical observables are

                                        Obscl (𝑈 ) = 𝐶CE
                                                      ∗
                                                         (L 𝑐 (𝑈 )) .

Disjoint union of supports gives the prefactorization product.

Theorem 104.1 (Factorization observables). An elliptic classical BV theory produces a factorization algebra
Obscl . A renormalized quantization satisfying the quantum master equation produces a factorization
algebra Obsq over 𝕜 [[ℏ]].
Proof. The Chevalley cochains of a direct sum are the completed tensor product of the cochains, giving the
disjoint-support multiplication. Elliptic descent supplies the Weiss cosheaf property. In the quantum theory,
renormalized time-ordered products define the structure maps, and the quantum master equation makes their
differential square-zero and compatible with nested ultraviolet contractions [19, 20].

104.2    Boundary and defect restriction
Let 𝑌 = 𝐶 × ℝ ≥0 , with 𝐶 a complex curve, and let a boundary condition be given at 𝐶 × {0}. Boundary
observables are holomorphic along 𝐶 and ordered along the inward normal line. A compact boundary
object 𝑏 in the boundary factorization category gives

                                            𝐴𝑏 = RHom( 𝑏, 𝑏 ) .

Theorem 104.2 (Ordered boundary algebra). When boundary observables are holomorphic on 𝐶 and
locally constant along the oriented normal direction, 𝐴𝑏 is an ordered chiral algebra

                                        𝐴𝑏 ∈ AlgE1 Fact𝐷 ( 𝐶 ) , ⊗! .
                                                                   


If 𝑏 is a local compact generator and the Morita equivalences obey factorization descent, the boundary
category is equivalent to 𝐴𝑏 -modules in factorization coeﬃcients.


                                                                                                          271
104. Local BV theories and factorization observables                                                      272


Proof. Composition of boundary endomorphisms gives the internal E1 multiplication. Holomorphic collision
gives the factorization coefficient on 𝐶 . The componentwise locality identity makes multiplication compatible
with every disjoint-support factorization map. Local compact-generator Morita theory descends over the Ran
diagrams. These are the recognition and Morita theorems of Volume I.

                   Three curve-level algebraic outputs
Let 𝐶 be a smooth curve and let D𝐶 be the fixed Ran category. The same geometric source can produce
three algebraic outputs, and their distinction is functorial.

Definition 105.1 (Curve-level faces). A transverse face is

                                         𝐴⊥ ∈ AlgE1 (Fact𝐷 ( 𝐶 ) , ⊗! ) .

An associative chiral face is
                                              𝐴ch                 ch
                                               Ass ∈ AlgE1 (D𝐶 , ★ ) .

A chiral Lie face is
                                              𝔤ch ∈ AlgLie (D𝐶 , ★ch ) .
Their bars are Bar⊥ , Barch           ch
                         Ass , and BarLie .

Theorem 105.2 (Source separation). A boundary normal direction determines the transverse face. A
chiral-convolution multiplication determines the associative chiral face. A Lie bracket for chiral convolution
determines the Lie face. The commutator functor sends the associative chiral face to the Lie face. A chiral
enveloping algebra 𝑈 ch (𝔤) satisﬁes

                                         Barch    ch         ch
                                            Ass (𝑈 (𝔤)) ≃ BarLie (𝔤)

on the nilcomplete PBW locus. A mixed source joins the transverse and associative chiral faces through an
aligned distributive law.
Proof. Each statement is the corresponding operadic restriction or adjunction in the named monoidal category.
The PBW equivalence is the enveloping lane of chiral Koszul duality. The mixed statement is the distributive-law
theorem of Volume I.

    This theorem replaces every dimension-based dispatch of an E𝑛 level. The geometry of the source
selects the available directions; the operadic level records the operations actually constructed.

Theorem 106.1 (Classical hCS factorization algebra). The action 𝑆hCS satisﬁes the classical master equa-

Proof. The Hamiltonian vector field is the Maurer–Cartan differential 𝜕¯ 𝐴 + 21 [ 𝐴, 𝐴]. Its square is the Dolbeault

                                                  Pushforward to a curve
Let 𝑝 : 𝑋 → 𝐶 be a holomorphic map to a complex curve. For a factorization algebra 𝔽 on 𝑋 , define

                                           ( 𝑝∗ 𝔽 ) (𝑈 ) = 𝔽 ( 𝑝 −1𝑈 ) .

Theorem 107.1 (Factorization pushforward). The assignment 𝑝∗ 𝔽 is a factorization algebra on 𝐶 . Suppose
in addition that 𝑝 is proper and locally trivial in a topology compatible with the factorization theory, that
𝔽 is locally constant in the ﬁber directions, and that product-chart excision and base change hold. Then on
every trivializing open 𝑈 ⊂ 𝐶 with ﬁber 𝐹𝑝 there is a natural equivalence
                                                          ∫
                                          ( 𝑝∗ 𝔽 ) (𝑈 ) ≃   𝔽 | 𝐹𝑝 ×𝑈 ,
                                                            𝐹𝑝

compatible on overlaps. If the resulting object is holomorphic and regular holonomic along 𝐶 , Riemann–
Hilbert algebraization gives a factorization 𝐷 -module on 𝐶 .
Proof. Disjoint opens in 𝐶 have disjoint inverse images, so the factorization products push forward. Weiss descent
is inherited from inverse images of Weiss covers. On a product chart, local constancy in the fiber and factoriza-
tion excision identify the value with fiberwise factorization homology. The base-change hypothesis glues these
local equivalences. Regular-holonomic Riemann–Hilbert transports the holomorphic factorization object to the
algebraic 𝐷 -module setting.

  Thus a curve shadow is attached to an actual map and an actual field theory. Different fibrations and
boundary conditions produce different ordered chiral algebras from the same categorical source.

    The derived centre and the physical bulk
For an ordered boundary algebra 𝐴, define

                                    𝑍 ord ( 𝐴) = RHom 𝐴⊗ 𝐴op ( 𝐴, 𝐴) .

It is a canonical E2 algebra. A bulk theory acting on the boundary gives

                                      𝛽 : Obsbulk | 𝜕 −→ 𝑍 ord ( 𝐴) .

Definition 108.1 (Open–closed completeness). A boundary condition is open–closed complete on a
protected sector when 𝛽 is an equivalence on that sector.

   The definition turns the phrase ‘‘bulk equals centre’’ into a comparison theorem. The centre exists
algebraically for every ordered boundary algebra; the physical bulk is identified with it by the open–closed
map.

Theorem 109.1 (Hall associativity). Assume a coeﬃcient theory in which 𝑝∗ and 𝑞∗ are deﬁned and satisfy
base change for the two-step ﬁltration stack. Then

                                           𝑎 ∗ 𝑏 = 𝑞∗ 𝑝 ∗ ( 𝑎 ⊠ 𝑏 )

deﬁnes an associative product.
Proof. The two parenthesizations are the two projections from the stack of filtrations 0 ⊂ 𝐸1 ⊂ 𝐸2 ⊂ 𝐸3 . Base
change identifies both composites with pullback to this stack followed by pushforward to 𝐸3 .

   For critical CoHAs, the coefficient theory includes vanishing cycles and a coherent orientation. For
symplectic CoHAs, it includes the selected equivariant Borel–Moore theory. Completion is chosen so
that each output degree receives a convergent sum.




                                                                                                           283
                                                                                       CHAPTER
                                                                                                                110
      Orientation data and the threefold Hall
                                     product
For compact Calabi–Yau threefolds, natural orientation data on moduli of coherent sheaves and perfect
complexes is constructed by Joyce and Upmeier [49]. A general cohomological Hall algebra for a 3-Calabi–
Yau category uses strong orientation data, which carries the coherent multiplicativity required by the
attractor and extension correspondences [52].

Theorem 110.1 (Threefold Hall algebra from strong orientation). Let C be a 3-Calabi–Yau category
satisfying the geometric hypotheses of Kinjo–Park–Safronov, and assume strong orientation data. Then its
cohomological Donaldson–Thomas complexes carry an associative cohomological Hall product. The product
is the parabolic-induction correspondence supplied by the attractor Lagrangian correspondence.
Proof. Kinjo–Park–Safronov prove the functoriality of the Donaldson–Thomas perverse sheaf under the attractor
Lagrangian correspondence and use strong orientation to define the parabolic-induction morphism. Their base-
change and associativity theorem gives the CoHA [52].

Definition 110.2 (Reduced 𝐾 3 × 𝐸 Hall datum). A reduced 𝐾 3 × 𝐸 Hall datum is a strong-oriented
threefold Hall datum together with descent along the elliptic translation action, a reduced deformation
theory, and a separated completion in physical charge. A full quantum-group datum additionally includes
a coproduct, Cartan extension, negative half, and nondegenerate continuous Hopf pairing.

   The geometric 𝐸 -equivariant Hall source
Let 𝑆 be a projective K3 surface, let 𝐸 be an elliptic curve, and put

                                                   𝑋 = 𝑆 × 𝐸.

Write Coh ≤1 ( 𝑋 ) for coherent sheaves whose support has dimension at most one. This is an extension-
closed abelian category. Its numerical charge group is denoted Γ𝑋 , and the degree of the projected
one-cycle on 𝐸 is the additive function

                                               𝑚 : Γ𝑋 −→ ℤ ≥0 .

For 𝛾 ∈ Γ𝑋 , let M 𝛾 be the moduli stack of objects of charge 𝛾 . Translation on the elliptic factor gives
an action of 𝐸 on every M 𝛾 and on every extension stack.
    Oberdieck and Shen construct the 𝐸 -equivariant Grothendieck ring of stacks and its Hall convolution.
In the present notation the completed algebra is

                                       H𝑋𝐸,mot = c 𝛾 ∈ Γ 𝐾0𝐸 (St/M 𝛾 ) ,
                                                 M
                                                           𝑋


where the completion is taken in effective curve class and Euler characteristic.

Theorem 111.1 (Geometric reduced Hall algebra). The extension correspondence in Coh ≤1 ( 𝑋 ) deﬁnes
an associative 𝐸 -equivariant motivic Hall product on H𝑋𝐸,mot . The Oberdieck–Shen reduced integration
morphism
                                  𝐼𝑋𝐸 : H𝑋𝐸,mot −→ ℚ[ \Γ𝑋 ] [ 𝜖 ]/( 𝜖 2 )
is multiplicative on the regular subalgebra on which their integration theorem is deﬁned. The coeﬃcient of
𝜖 is the reduced contribution of the positive-dimensional 𝐸 -orbits.
Proof. The stack of short exact sequences is 𝐸 -equivariant because translation preserves exactness and numerical
charge. The stack of two-step filtrations identifies the two composites of three Hall products, so equivariant base
change proves associativity. The integration statement is the 𝐸 -equivariant motivic Hall theorem of Oberdieck–
Shen [65]. Their stabilizer stratification separates fixed contributions from moving elliptic orbits and packages the
latter in the square-zero parameter 𝜖 .
